# EPIC-003 — Research agents and their conversation interface

## Description

Deliver Ohtia's first working agents and the shared conversation module they use. The researcher connects a ChatGPT subscription through Sign in with ChatGPT, an Anthropic API key or an OpenAI API key; creates a project by conversing with Virgilio; and then works with Virgilio and Josefa in project-scoped conversations. Virgilio guides directly, consults Josefa, proposes bounded plans that the researcher approves, synthesizes their results, hands a conversation to Josefa and keeps an automatic, inspectable project memory. Every request runs as a durable, bounded task with explicit limits, attributable results and honest uncertainty.[^roadmap][^collaboration]

The roadmap places the common interaction module, durable interventions and tasks, contribution graphs, planning/contribution/synthesis turns, handoffs, project memory, model preferences and provider routes in this EPIC.[^roadmap] The refinement decided every story, value and amendment with the researcher who owns the product, one story at a time.

## Functional and technical specification

### SPEC-003-001: Purpose, scope and exclusions

This EPIC makes Ohtia useful for the first time: without a connected language-model service the application can only show its interface. It delivers the provider connections, the durable task engine, the project setup conversation, the chat panel, two working agents and the coordination module that later specialists reuse.[^roadmap]

Product principles keep their exact meanings: PRIN-002 researcher authorship, PRIN-004 provenance, PRIN-006 uncertainty and PRIN-016 honest capability limits. They appear here as concrete rules: an agent never records a decision the researcher did not take, every agent message keeps its producer, unknown usage is never zero and unavailable capabilities say so.

Working agents in this EPIC:

- Virgilio: project setup (`project_setup`), direct guidance (`direct_guidance`, which also proposes consultations, plans, handoffs, plan changes and memory updates) and plan synthesis (`contribution_synthesis`).
- Josefa: search strategy planning (`search_strategy_planning`), as a written plan in the conversation.

Jorge Luis, Erasmus and the writing-review modes (Boris and María) appear in the agent selector as unavailable. Thomas does not appear in the selector. Their role definitions exist; their operations do not.[^roadmap][^identity]

Project types: only `thesis` exists. Every name, table and instruction is generic over the project type, so later types add a value and a description instead of new tables or operations.

Excluded from this EPIC, with no work assigned elsewhere by this document:

- archiving or deleting conversations, recorded as TD-003 in `docs/technical-debt.md`;
- deleting projects and changing a project's title or document language after setup;
- executed search, search adapters and durable search-strategy records;
- operations for Jorge Luis, Erasmus, Boris, María and Thomas, and an agent profile view;
- contributor portraits and animated gestures; conversations identify each agent by its static role symbol, name, role and AI label through the existing `VoiceLabel`, the "distinct static avatar or role symbol with name and role" that `agent-identity.md` allows;[^identity]
- operating-system notifications, sounds, Dock or taskbar badges and an inbox;
- the diagnostics viewer and diagnostic export;
- the Codex runtime route and subscription access through a locally signed-in `claude` command: the researcher chose Sign in with ChatGPT, OpenAI's official program for applications, over driving a local Codex runtime, and rejected launching the researcher's `claude` command;
- automatic fallback to another provider, account or model, including Anthropic's server-side refusal fallbacks;
- agent-quality evaluation gates, including comparisons against a single-agent baseline: the researcher decided that Ohtia does not grade the models the researcher connects;
- collaboration scenarios 13 and 14, which need the manuscript editor;
- source or manuscript actions that open a conversation, because this EPIC has no source or manuscript surfaces;
- choosing a model per message, selecting a subset of a proposed plan and editing a proposal before accepting it.

### SPEC-003-002: Cross-cutting delivery rules

These rules apply to every story in this EPIC.

- **Delivery unit.** Each story is one issue, one pull request and one signed commit, after the review gate in `AGENTS.md`. A story is closable once its declared prerequisites hold.
- **Automated tests.** Every story carries its own automated tests and runs them in all 12 CI cells: Ubuntu 26.04 GNOME and KDE on x86-64 and ARM64, Fedora 44 GNOME and KDE on x86-64, Debian 13 GNOME and KDE on x86-64, Windows on x86-64 and ARM64, and macOS 26 and 27 on ARM64.
- **Real-account tests.** Tests with the researcher's own accounts run only on the researcher's x86-64 virtual machines: Windows, Ubuntu 26.04, Fedora 44 and Debian 13. These are the supported Linux distributions; Fedora adds SELinux and Btrfs, and Debian 13 adds glibc 2.41 and a GNOME Keyring with a known first-login defect. macOS and ARM64 are covered by CI only, because the researcher's test machines are x86-64 virtual machines. The researcher signs in to ChatGPT in the development runtime, whose credential-store entries are separate from the installed application's and belong to one checkout (STORY-003-001): in STORY-003-002, once on the Windows virtual machine and once in each Linux desktop, because GNOME and KDE keep separate Secret Service stores. Every new checkout has its own entries and signs in once: each story's own worktree asks for one sign-in on each of these machines and desktops before its first real-account check, and for the researcher's API keys again before a test of STORY-003-036 or STORY-003-037 uses them. Within one checkout, later runs reuse its stored session, and the implementing agent runs the development commands with it. Paid API-key tests (STORY-003-036 and STORY-003-037) use the researcher's keys and start only after the researcher sets a spending cap in the provider console.
- **Development commands.** Commands that exercise providers before the interface exists are command-line switches of the `development` runtime mode of ADR-0043, the unpackaged runtime that `python3 scripts/workspace.py run dev` starts with its own state under `<checkout>/.ohtia-dev/`.[^adr-0043] No package contains them: not the development packages built in CI, not internal candidates and not releases. SPEC-003-008 defines the switches, their actions and the check that proves their absence.
- **Time-limit measurement.** In every real-account test, record each reply's duration and its longest silence between provider events. If a real reply uses more than half of its operation's attempt duration, or a silence exceeds half of `max_inactivity_ms`, that limit is raised, and where the raise lands depends on the measured version. When the story introduces that version, it raises the value in its own block before it merges, without a new version. When the version is already merged, the story records the measurement and the raised value in its pull request, and the raise lands in that operation's next version planned in SPEC-003-004 that has not merged yet, whose story applies it before it closes, so the planned version numbers stay as they are. When no such version is planned, the measuring story adds, before it closes, a new policy-only version with the raised value, which takes the next policy version and keeps the instruction version (SPEC-003-004). STORY-003-031, which runs beside the `direct_guidance` version chain, is the second case: any raise its measurements require lands in `direct_guidance` version 6 of STORY-003-032, which closes only after those measurements exist. A version that raises a limit of an operation that a plan or a consultation reserves (STORY-003-024) also adds the next plan or consultation limits policy version, whose totals keep 8 contributions with two attempts each and the synthesis for a plan, or two of Josefa's task reservations for a consultation; the story that applies the raise adds it before it closes. Limits are fixed and versioned; the researcher cannot change them.
- **Accessibility and locales.** Every story with interface work applies the roadmap's shared rule for languages and accessibility.[^roadmap] Its surfaces meet WCAG 2.2 AA and ADR-0023's eleven selected AAA criteria (1.4.6, 1.4.8, 2.1.3, 2.2.4, 2.2.5, 2.2.6, 2.4.8, 2.4.10, 2.4.12, 2.4.13 and 3.3.6), with ATAG 2.0 Part B as authoring guidance.[^adr-0023] Each such story checks keyboard-only operation with focus order and restoration; names, roles, states and announcements with a screen reader; a 280 CSS px chat width wherever it changes the chat panel, and reflow at 320 CSS px; light and dark themes; forced colors; reduced motion; German as the long translation, Korean and mixed scripts in the packaged fonts, and pseudolocalization; the common locale service's ordered operating-system fallback and Settings override; and the native input methods (IBus Hangul on Linux, the Microsoft Korean keyboard on Windows) in every text field it adds. Each manual screen-reader result names the artifact, platform and journey actually run; automated checks do not establish conformance. New interface text enters the typed catalogs of every supported locale under ADR-0024; the English strings in this EPIC are the catalog source text.[^adr-0024] Placeholders in braces, such as `{service}`, `{agent}` and `{n}`, are named catalog parameters, each defined where a block first uses it; numbers, dates, times, durations and lists are formatted for the interface locale, never built by joining parts. A text marked "with plural forms" is an ICU plural message on the count it names; the first story that merges one adds the typed plural rendering contract that `docs/localization.md` requires, and later stories reuse it. Each count has its own plural selector; a part omitted at zero is an `=0` branch of the same message. Catalog text uses the typographic apostrophe ’ (U+2019) and quotation marks “ ”, as `en-US.json` does; this EPIC writes them as straight characters for readability. Every button label and every icon button's accessible name is a verb with its object or complement, such as "Confirm setup" or "Continue with Josefa", except "New project" and "New conversation", "Try again" wherever an action is repeated, and the Preference saving labels "Check", "Choose again" and "Apply again", the exceptions that `design-system.md` records (STORY-003-015 and STORY-003-018). The safe option of every confirmation dialog is "Close dialog", which Esc also runs and which takes the initial focus; from STORY-003-009 on, the existing `dialog.close` catalog text, which the header close button of every dialog and the footer close buttons of the About and Help dialogs use, also reads "Close dialog", and the button that closes the Settings layout notice takes its own text, "Close notice" (STORY-003-009).
- **Notices.** Every notice of this EPIC holds only its icon and text inside its box. Its buttons and links sit below the box, `space.2` under it, on the surrounding panel or page surface, never inside the box, as `research-presentation.md` requires: "Attention notices contain only attention text on their surface; their actions and focus indicators sit on the surrounding page surface."[^research-presentation]
- **Long lists.** No list loads more items because the researcher scrolled. Earlier messages, older conversations and more projects load only through their buttons, "Load earlier messages" (STORY-003-010), "Load older conversations" (STORY-003-018) and "Show more projects" (STORY-003-016), as `design-system.md` requires: "Long lists are paginated or loaded in batches through an explicit action; scrolling does not load content without notice."[^design]
- **Diagnostics.** Every story adds catalog events for its own operations to the ADR-0013 `ohtia-log-v1` catalog, with its privacy review. Diagnostics never contain prompts, responses, tool arguments, titles, memory text, credentials or provider error messages.[^adr-0013]
- **Credentials and provider requests.** Main holds every credential: the Sign in with ChatGPT session and registration, the Anthropic API key and the OpenAI API key. Main also performs every provider HTTP request: the ChatGPT Responses transport, the Anthropic SDK and the OpenAI Responses transport. The data service runs the task engine: it decides what is sent and when, builds each request's data without credentials, and stores the events and results that main forwards; SPEC-003-008 defines how a dispatch crosses between them. No credential ever enters the data service, the database with its write-ahead log and ADR-0010 checkpoints, diagnostics, error notices, command-line arguments, `command_receipts` or "Details". A pasted key passes through the window once, from a masked field to main, and never returns to the window.[^architecture]
- **Honest values.** Unknown usage, model or effort is recorded as unknown, never as zero or as a guess. No layer adds hidden retries. Ohtia never switches provider, account or model by itself.[^adr-0029]
- **Agent instructions.** Agent instructions are written in English. Agents answer in the project's `document_language`. Every task records its operation, policy version and instruction version.
- **Operations and command receipts.** Port operations and data-service commands are named `<record>.<action>`, both parts in camelCase, as the existing `locale.replace` and `layout.receipt` are. Each block names the operations its story adds. Every data-changing command carries an operation identifier, and the names of these commands are the values the data service accepts in `command_receipts.command`; each story adds its own to that list. Reads, text parts and the research subscription (STORY-003-010) write no receipt. Connection operations are not data-service commands: main handles them, they are idempotent by state and they record no `command_receipts` row (STORY-003-001). The execution-limit preference keeps its own receipt table, as the existing preferences do (STORY-003-019). From STORY-003-010 on, the application port carries every research operation that a block of this EPIC names: STORY-003-010 exposes the operations of STORY-003-004, STORY-003-012 exposes `task.stop` of STORY-003-007, STORY-003-013 exposes `usagePause.resume`, `task.startAgain` and `task.continueAll` of STORY-003-006 to STORY-003-008, the changed `project.confirmSetup` and the new `project.rejectSetup` of STORY-003-014, which can merge before or after STORY-003-010, reach the port with whichever of those two stories merges second, and every later story exposes the operations its own block adds. A story that exposes an operation also adds its error codes to the port (SPEC-003-015). The exceptions are the `development.*` commands, which only the development command calls, `task.cancelAll`, which main sends (STORY-003-009), and `plan.addContributions`, a data-service command that only tests and the development command call (STORY-003-023), so the window can never add work to an accepted plan that the researcher did not see on a card.
- **Documentation amendments.** Each canonical document change listed in SPEC-003-005 lands in the pull request of the story that introduces it. An amended document gets the story's `generated.by` and loses its `verified` declaration, which the researcher adds again after reviewing it, as `AGENTS.md` requires. A new ADR takes the next free number of `docs/adr/index.md` when its story integrates and is added to that index in the same pull request; this EPIC writes `ADR-NNNN` for that number.[^architecture-decisions]
- **Migrations.** Every schema change runs through the existing ADR-0010 checkpoint and recovery path. Stories that can run at the same time take their migration number when they integrate: the second to merge renumbers its migration and updates `manifest.json` and the storage revision list.[^adr-0010]
- **Comparisons.** Each design was compared with Paperclip, Multica and OpenClaw; SPEC-003-003 records what was adopted and what was rejected.
- **Other ADRs that apply.** ADR-0000: each story that changes a security boundary (credentials, outbound requests, rendering or port operations) records the applicable OWASP ASVS 5.0.0 L2 requirement IDs, their applicability and evidence in its pull request. These stories are the ones that hold credentials or send provider requests (STORY-003-001, STORY-003-002, STORY-003-003, STORY-003-035, STORY-003-036 and STORY-003-037), that change how message text is rendered (STORY-003-010 and STORY-003-011), and that change the application port's or main's broker operations (STORY-003-001, STORY-003-010, STORY-003-011, STORY-003-013, STORY-003-019 and STORY-003-036); later stories add research commands within the port section that STORY-003-010 defines. ADR-0027: every operation receives a per-attempt input built from immutable records, and agent tools are bound to project, attempt and expected versions through the closed `ohtia` tool registry; no adapter inherits configuration from environment variables. ADR-0036: the concurrency limit (STORY-003-019) and the agent model preferences (STORY-003-035) are typed, versioned application preference records in the data service. ADR-0015 governs parsers for imported content; this EPIC imports no files, and model replies are parsed in the renderer under ADR-0014. The data service never parses Markdown. ADR-0012's per-attempt process capability does not apply, because this EPIC starts no worker process: the task engine runs in the data service and provider requests run in main, both trusted components, and a model can only return text and tool calls as data.[^adr-0012] ADR-0012's other rules still hold in the data service: an attempt's identity comes from its own record, never from model arguments; events and results for an ended data-service generation, a replaced attempt or a cancelled or uncertain attempt are discarded; and a tool call only creates a proposal that the researcher confirms or rejects in the interface.
- **License notices.** Each story that adds a dependency adds its license notice to About's third-party notices, as the existing dependencies do.
- **Repository starting points** at the pinned base revision. Extend these boundaries rather than create parallel ones:
  - data service: `packages/utility/src/entry.ts` (including its `shutdown` message handler, the data service's orderly stop), `storage.ts` (the ADR-0010 checkpoint), `storage-lifecycle.ts` and `migrations/` with `manifest.json`;
  - main process: `packages/main/src/entry.ts` (the data-service `generation` and `before-quit`), `shutdown.ts` (`stopBrokerServices`, called from `before-quit`), `port-admission.ts` and `diagnostic-writer.ts`;
  - contracts and preload: `packages/contracts/src/port.ts`, `commands.ts` (command registry and shortcuts) and `diagnostics.ts` (event catalog), and `packages/preload/src/foundation-port.ts`;
  - renderer: `packages/renderer/src/index.tsx` (export barrel), `workspace-chrome.tsx` (`WorkspaceChrome`: context bar, panel buttons, explorer, welcome and chat panel texts), `settings-dialog.tsx`, `contributor-identity.tsx` (`VoiceLabel` and `ContributorSymbol`), `research-presentation.tsx` (the research components, beside which STORY-003-015 adds `ActionProposalCard`), `command-discovery.tsx` (command palette) and `design-tokens.css`;
  - configuration: `config/desktop/resource-policy.json` and `config/desktop/diagnostic-cases.json`;
  - new: the provider adapters, the task engine and the coordination module are new modules. The credential service and each adapter's HTTP transport live in `packages/main`; the task engine, each adapter's request building and the coordination module live in `packages/utility`; the adapter contract and the messages between main and the data service live in `packages/contracts`, as versioned, runtime-validated messages under ADR-0001. The coordination module is Ohtia's own; no agent framework such as LangGraph is adopted.

### SPEC-003-003: Reference systems and how they informed the design

Paperclip, Multica and OpenClaw were read at pinned commits as reference implementations; no code was copied.[^paperclip][^multica][^openclaw]

- **Task engine.** All three run several tasks at once and order work per conversation. Ohtia adopted one running task per conversation, coalescing of waiting messages and closed failure codes, and a general limit of 10, chosen by the researcher, under the provider's declared limit (ChatGPT declares 3).
- **Restart.** All three resume automatically because they are servers. Ohtia is a desktop application, so it resumes automatically only after an orderly exit and waits for the researcher's "Continue all ({n})" after a crash or power loss, as OpenClaw separates the two cases.
- **Exit.** None of the three has an exit dialog; the "Wait for replies, then quit" option comes from ADR-0011.
- **Consultation and delegation.** All three end the requesting agent's turn and wake it later. Ohtia does the same, but asks the researcher before any work is sent to another agent, like OpenClaw's optional "ask" mode, always on.
- **Dependencies.** Paperclip's explicit blockers, where a cancelled blocker does not count as done, shaped contribution dependencies.
- **Model selection.** Paperclip and Multica choose the runtime or adapter first and the model second; Ohtia uses the same two steps (service, then model). OpenClaw's automatic model fallback was rejected.
- **Coordinator.** Ohtia builds its own coordinator instead of adopting LangGraph or another agent framework, so durable records, limits and the researcher's approvals stay in Ohtia's own database.
- **Memory.** OpenClaw and Claude Code let the agent write memory files automatically;[^openclaw][^claude-code-memory] Ohtia's memory is also automatic, but every entry keeps its producer and sources, and agent text can never become a researcher decision.
- **Usage.** Paperclip holds accounting open until usage receipts arrive; Ohtia keeps a bounded 15-minute late-report window and leaves missing usage unknown.

### SPEC-003-004: Operation policies

Every operation has a versioned policy with all ADR-0011 fields plus `max_inactivity_ms`, the longest time without any provider event before the attempt stops.[^adr-0011] The fields are `max_pending_tasks`, `max_input_bytes`, `max_input_items`, `max_output_bytes`, `max_work_units`, `max_attempt_duration_ms`, `max_inactivity_ms`, `stop_grace_ms`, `max_attempts`, `max_auto_retries` and `retry_backoff_ms`. Values are fixed per merged policy version, recorded with each task and never editable by the researcher; the measurement rule in SPEC-003-002 is the only way they change. Work units count one per attempt whose request was dispatched; an attempt that never dispatched spends nothing.

Each operation's values live in the specification block of the story that introduces or changes it. A new version keeps every value of the previous version unless its block states otherwise. In this EPIC an operation's policy version and instruction version advance together, so "`direct_guidance` version 3" names both. The one exception is a policy-only version that the measurement rule adds for an operation with no later version planned below: it takes the next policy version, keeps the instruction version and changes only the raised limit. Operations in this EPIC, with their owning stories:

- `development_test`: STORY-003-003, the `development` runtime mode only.
- `project_setup`: STORY-003-014.
- `direct_guidance` version 1: STORY-003-017.
- `search_strategy_planning` version 1: STORY-003-020; version 2 (consultations and plans): STORY-003-022; version 3 (handoff): STORY-003-029; version 4 (memory): STORY-003-032.
- `direct_guidance` version 2 (consultation): STORY-003-022.
- Consultation and plan work limits: STORY-003-024.
- `contribution_synthesis` version 1: STORY-003-026; version 2 (plan changes): STORY-003-030; version 3 (memory): STORY-003-032.
- `direct_guidance` versions 3 (plans), 4 (handoff), 5 (plan changes) and 6 (memory): STORY-003-027, STORY-003-029, STORY-003-030 and STORY-003-032.

- `config/desktop/resource-policy.json` sign-in wait, the budget `sign_in_wait_ms`: STORY-003-002.

- `docs/agents/agent-collaboration.md` "Specialist selection and routing" and scenario 17 of `docs/agents/agent-collaboration-evaluation.md`: unavailable roles are shown disabled, without a named alternative; the `docs/design-system.md` `check` mapping, which also marks the chosen agent in a selector; and the `docs/workspace-layout.md` agent band of the chat panel: STORY-003-021.

- `docs/agents/agent-collaboration.md` "Completion, cancellation and recovery": a plan can be complete while an optional contribution ended uncertain, which it reports as uncertain; and its "Intervention and contribution records" rule on duplicate contribution requests: the policy-versioned key is scoped to the intervention, over agent, operation, request and prerequisites: STORY-003-023.
- `docs/agents/agent-collaboration.md` "Shared work envelope": a policy may set zero coordinator turns or dependencies when its interventions never use them, as a single consultation does: STORY-003-024.
- `docs/agents/agent-collaboration.md` recovery: a plan's waiting work continues by itself after an orderly exit: STORY-003-025.
- `docs/agents/agent-virgilio.md` planning within direct guidance and synthesis as its own operation, including its sentence that planning, direct guidance and synthesis each need their own evaluated policy, the `docs/agents/agent-collaboration.md` planning-turn sentence and coordinator diagram and the `docs/product-roadmap.md` "separately bounded planning" wording: STORY-003-027.
- `docs/design-system.md` icon mappings of agent-work state labels, with the rule that a state label never reuses an icon mapped to another meaning: STORY-003-028.
- `docs/agents/agent-collaboration.md` handoff: what a proposed handoff identifies, the owner changes without an intervention or a shared budget, and the recipient's replies are ordinary tasks; and the `docs/agents/agent-virgilio.md` "Plan bounded collaboration" output, a proposed handoff with its note: STORY-003-029.
- `docs/agents/agent-collaboration.md` "Handoff ownership and redirection": a researcher redirection may alter work that has not completed within the accepted envelope, running work it touches is stopped and superseded first, and completion, readiness, dependencies and progress count only the current contributions: STORY-003-030.
- `docs/design-system.md` `ActivityBadge`, with its corner look and measurements, and its "Shape" rule that also allows `radius.pill` for the `ActivityBadge` count, and `docs/workspace-layout.md` panel marks: STORY-003-031.
- `docs/design-system.md` icon mapping for memory changes: STORY-003-032.

### SPEC-003-006: Provider adapter contract and credential store

Implement the ADR-0026 adapter contract shared by every provider, with its fake provider and conformance suite, the connection state and operations in main, and the operating-system credential store that every connection uses.[^adr-0026]

Generic contract:

- **Declaration.** Each provider declares its identity and version, its capabilities and the sign-in modes it supports. Each connection also declares its identifier, a fixed code of lowercase letters and underscores that main reports to the data service and that `tasks.connection` and `usage_limit_pauses.connection` store (`chatgpt` in STORY-003-002, `anthropic_api_key` and `openai_api_key` in STORY-003-036 and STORY-003-037, and `fake` for the fake provider); its display name, which every interface text shows as `{service}` ("ChatGPT" for Sign in with ChatGPT, "Anthropic" and "OpenAI" for the two API-key connections of STORY-003-036 and STORY-003-037); its kind of connection, `subscription` (a plan the researcher pays for and uses through sign-in) or `api_key` (usage charged per token to the key's account), which selects the variant of every text whose meaning depends on how the service charges; and, when the service has one, the address where the researcher checks usage (`https://chatgpt.com/settings/usage` for ChatGPT; the API-key connections declare none). No interface text names a fixed service.
- **Connection states.** `disconnected`, `connecting`, `connected`, `expired` and `error`. Only `connected` lets tasks run; the data service treats the other four as unavailable (STORY-003-006).
- **Connection reason.** `connection.read` and every `connection.subscribe` event return, with the state, a `reason` from a closed list, empty unless the state names it: `store_unavailable` (state `error`: the credential store is missing, or its unlock was cancelled), `sign_in_timeout` (state `disconnected`: the browser sign-in exceeded its wait), `plan_use_not_allowed` (state `error`: the account refused or lacks plan use) and `sign_in_rejected` (state `error`: the service rejected the sign-in, its grant or the registration). Main finds `store_unavailable` each time it reads the store. A reason for a kept session is stored with that session, so it survives a restart until a new sign-in replaces the session. Any other reason lives only in main's memory, until the next `connection.connect` or `connection.disconnect` or until Ohtia quits; the next start then reads the state from the stored session. STORY-003-002 and STORY-003-003 set each reason, and STORY-003-036 and STORY-003-037 use `store_unavailable`.
- **Connection operations.** `connection.read`, `connection.subscribe`, `connection.probe` (the "Test connection" and "Refresh {service} model list" buttons), `connection.listModels` (Model list on the port, below), `connection.connect` and `connection.disconnect`, carried through the application port and handled by main, which holds the credentials. They are idempotent by state and record no `command_receipts` row: connecting while `connecting` or `connected` returns the current state, and disconnecting while `disconnected` returns `disconnected`. Connect returns `connecting` at once and later states arrive through `connection.subscribe`, so the port's request deadline never has to cover a browser sign-in. Probe also returns at once, `connection.read` and its events carry `probing: true` until it ends, and its result arrives through `connection.subscribe` as one probe-result event that carries the outcome, `listed` or `unreachable`, and the connection's `model_list_revision`, never the list itself. Main ends a probe without an answer after the new budget `connection_probe_ms`, 30 seconds, as `unreachable`; a probe requested while one runs returns the running one.
- **Model list on the port.** Main keeps each connection's latest complete model list with its `model_list_revision`, which starts at 1 with the first list main stores for that connection after it starts and rises by one each time main stores a new complete list; `connection.read` and every `connection.subscribe` event carry it, empty while the connection has no list, with the list's number of models. The window reads the list with the new read `connection.listModels`, given the connection identifier, the `model_list_revision` it holds, a cursor, the position of the next entry, 0 for the first page, and a count of at most 128. It returns, in the list's order, as many whole entries as fit within every bound of the port (the connection operation's `initial_output_bytes`, 128 items per container, 512 values per message and 4,096 bytes per string), up to that count, with the next cursor and whether more entries exist. When the revision it was given is no longer the connection's, it returns the existing `stale_revision` with `currentRevision` set to the current one, and the window reads the list again from the first page with that revision; `operationErrorCodes` lists `stale_revision` for `connection.listModels`. A list with an entry that would not fit alone in one page is a list main cannot parse, so main never stores it. Every view that shows a connection's model list from main reads every page, so it renders the complete list.
- **Main and the data service.** Main reports each connection's identifier, state and model list to the data service over the service channel, never its credential; the data service blocks and resumes tasks by that state (STORY-003-006). The data service never receives a connection operation.
- **Fake provider and conformance suite.** A fake provider implements the contract, and a conformance suite that every adapter must pass checks declarations, states, operations and failures. The fake provider exists only in tests and in the `development` runtime mode of ADR-0043: it lives in its own module, `packages/main/src/fake-provider.ts`, which main loads only in that mode and in tests; the `installed` and `qualification` modes never declare the `fake` connection, and outside tests Settings › Connections never lists it. An artifact inspection test fails if any package built in the 12 CI cells contains that module, as the development command's absence is proved (STORY-003-003).[^adr-0043]
- **No window text.** This story adds no interface: the fake provider is its only connection. Sign in with ChatGPT and its Settings › Connections rows are STORY-003-002; the API-key connections are STORY-003-036 and STORY-003-037.

Credential service, in main, using `@napi-rs/keyring` 2.1.2 with `linux: { store: 'secret-service' }` so that Linux fails instead of falling back to a non-persistent store.[^keyring] It holds the Sign in with ChatGPT session and registration that STORY-003-002 obtains and the API keys of STORY-003-036 and STORY-003-037:

- **macOS.** The whole session is stored in the Keychain.
- **Linux.** The whole session is stored in GNOME Keyring or KWallet through the Secret Service. With no store, the credential service reports the store as unavailable and stores nothing. With a locked store, the system unlock prompt appears; if the researcher cancels it, the credential service reports the store as unavailable in the same way. Settings › Connections explains it (STORY-003-002 and STORY-003-036).
- **Windows.** A random 32-byte key is stored in Credential Manager, whose credentials hold at most 2,560 bytes.[^windows-credential] The session is encrypted with AES-256-GCM through `node:crypto` in `provider-sessions.enc` directly inside Electron's `userData` directory (`app.getPath("userData")`), outside the research data directory and its ADR-0010 checkpoints. Every write uses a fresh random 12-byte IV, and the file holds, in this order, the format version byte `1`, that IV, the ciphertext and the 16-byte authentication tag; main verifies the tag before it uses any decrypted byte. Only main writes it, through a temporary file in the same directory that then replaces it. A missing file reads as no stored session, so the connection is `disconnected`: this is the state on a first run and after "Sign out of {service}", which deletes the file. A file that exists but whose key is missing, or that fails to decrypt, including one with another version byte, one shorter than the version byte, IV and tag, and one whose tag does not verify, reads as an unusable session, and the connection becomes `expired`. This follows OpenAI Codex's approach for the same limit.[^codex-keyring]
- **Entry names.** Each credential is one credential-store entry, named by a service and an account. The service is `Ohtia` in the `installed` and `qualification` runtime modes. In the `development` mode of ADR-0043 it is `Ohtia Development`, a space and the checkout identifier: the first 16 hexadecimal characters, in lowercase, of the SHA-256 of the UTF-8 bytes of the development root, the canonical absolute path of the checkout's `.ohtia-dev` directory, which main reads from `OHTIA_DEVELOPMENT_ROOT` and admits before it redirects `userData` into that root and calls `app.requestSingleInstanceLock()` in `packages/main/src/entry.ts`.[^adr-0043] So a development run never reads or overwrites the installed application's credentials or another checkout's, and no two processes ever share a stored session or registration: the `installed` and `qualification` modes use the native `userData` directory, so their single-instance lock admits one process per operating-system user, and each checkout's lock follows its own redirected `userData`, admitting one process per checkout, which the development command reaches through `second-instance` (STORY-003-003).[^architecture] A new checkout, or one moved to another path, has its own entries, so the researcher signs in once there and enters the API keys again; the entries of a removed or moved checkout stay in the store, and Ohtia never reads them again. The accounts are `chatgpt-session` (macOS and Linux: the whole session), `chatgpt-session-key` (Windows: the key of `provider-sessions.enc`, created only when the entry is missing and otherwise reused, so the checkout's session file stays readable across restarts), `chatgpt-registration`, and `anthropic-api-key` and `openai-api-key` (STORY-003-036 and STORY-003-037).
- **Never shown.** No credential appears in the interface, diagnostics or project data, and no message from main to the data service contains one.

**Documentation amendments:**

- `docs/architecture.md`, "Process and trust boundaries": after "Main validates the sender and envelope, owns native dialogs and brokered credentials, and supervises the application data service." add "For direct HTTP provider adapters, main also performs the provider requests: the data service's task engine admits each request and sends main its request data, without credentials, with a single-use dispatch permit; main attaches the credential, sends the request and returns the provider's events to the data service. No credential enters the data service."; in the diagram, add the edge `Main --> Providers`.
- `docs/architecture.md`, "Transfers, credentials and privacy": "Credentials managed by Ohtia use OS-backed protected storage when available; runtime-owned login remains owned by that runtime in its isolated configuration. Electron documents platform-dependent protection and a Linux plaintext fallback, so absence of a suitable protected backend must be detected. Offer session-only use or leave the connection unavailable; never silently persist secrets with weak fallback.", with its footnote reference to `electron-secrets`, becomes "Credentials managed by Ohtia live in the operating-system credential store through `@napi-rs/keyring`, held by main: the Keychain on macOS and the Secret Service on Linux hold them whole, and on Windows a random key in Credential Manager encrypts the session file `provider-sessions.enc`, which main keeps directly inside Electron's `userData` directory, outside the research data directory. Runtime-owned login remains owned by that runtime in its isolated configuration. A missing or unusable store, or a cancelled unlock, leaves the connection unavailable; Ohtia offers no session-only use and never persists secrets with a weaker fallback." The new sentence on `provider-sessions.enc` ends with a reference to the existing footnote `electron-paths`. The `electron-secrets` footnote definition and its entry in the frontmatter `sources` are removed, because no sentence cites them any more.
- `docs/architecture.md`, "Initial provider routes": "For OpenAI, use a local Codex app-server over stdio as the initial rich-integration route. Its documented surface supports threads, streamed turns, managed account login and approval requests; the adapter must check the pinned runtime protocol and account capabilities. Model access is tested separately from listing a catalog. Do not expose remote app-server listeners or make experimental fields a silent requirement.", with its footnote reference to `codex-app-server`, becomes "For OpenAI, the initial routes are Sign in with ChatGPT, OpenAI's sign-in program for applications, over the Responses API, and an OpenAI API key. Both are direct HTTP adapters whose requests main sends with the credential it holds. Model access is tested separately from listing a catalog."; the `codex-app-server` footnote definition and its entry in the frontmatter `sources` are removed; the Anthropic paragraph, which keeps API-key access as the baseline, stays as it is; and in the paragraph that follows it, "Both routes require version-specific conformance and a supported graphical setup path before release." becomes "Every route requires version-specific conformance and a supported graphical setup path before release." and the sentence "Optional direct model-API adapters can be introduced later without replacing the agent contract or claiming equivalent behavior." is removed.
- `docs/product-roadmap.md`, EPIC-003 entry: in the first stories, "Implement provider routes as independent conformance stories, using the architecture's initial Codex app-server stdio route and permitted Anthropic programmatic candidate after current access verification." becomes "Implement provider routes as independent conformance stories, using the architecture's initial routes: Sign in with ChatGPT over the Responses API, an Anthropic API key and an OpenAI API key."; in "Done when", "Credentials use protected storage or an explicit session-only mode." becomes "Credentials stay in the operating-system credential store; without a usable store the connection stays unavailable."; and in the readiness list, the EPIC-003 entry's "protected or session-only credentials" becomes "credentials in the operating-system credential store".
- `docs/application-port.md`, "Scope and authority": "The portable API carries application configuration only; it has no project, research, credential, filesystem, SQL, subprocess or provider authority." becomes "The portable API carries application configuration and the provider connection operations `connection.read`, `connection.subscribe`, `connection.probe`, `connection.listModels`, `connection.connect` and `connection.disconnect`, which main handles without forwarding them to the data service; it has no project, research, credential, filesystem, SQL or subprocess authority, and no operation returns a credential."
- `config/desktop/resource-policy.json`, keeping `policy_id` `foundation-1` because no existing value changes: the scope of `initial_input_bytes`, "Payload only for each initial status or appearance read/change operation", becomes "Payload only for each initial status or appearance read/change operation and each connection operation"; the scope of `initial_input_items`, "Total own fields in initial operation payload", becomes "Total own fields in each initial or connection operation payload"; the scope of `initial_output_bytes`, "Payload only for each initial operation result", becomes "Payload only for each initial or connection operation result or event"; the scope of `initial_work_units`, "One status snapshot, preference read or atomic appearance replacement per request", becomes "One status snapshot, preference read, atomic appearance replacement or connection operation per request"; the scope of `request_deadline_ms`, "Status and preference requests from admission to reply; includes queues", becomes "Status, preference and connection requests from admission to reply; includes queues"; and the scope of `subscriptions_per_window`, "Active status or preference subscriptions in one window", becomes "All active subscriptions in one window". Add the budget `connection_probe_ms` with `value` 30000, `unit` `millisecond`, permitted minimum and maximum 30000, `owner` `003`, scope "One connection probe, from its request until its result event", failure `probe_unreachable` and rationale "A model list arrives well within this time; a slower answer is reported as unreachable". `scripts/desktop_contract.py` gains the name and its unit.
- Add `docs/adr/adr-NNNN-provider-access-routes-and-credential-store.md`, titled "ADR-NNNN — Provider access routes and credential store", with the description "Connect language-model services through Sign in with ChatGPT and Anthropic and OpenAI API keys, with every credential held by main in the operating-system credential store." Its context states that Ohtia needs subscription and API-key access without a local agent runtime, that `architecture.md` gives main the brokered credentials, and that the researcher rejected a session-only fallback. Its decision records the three routes, main as the only holder of credentials and sender of provider requests, the credential store per operating system with the Windows session file and its format, the entry names with the `Ohtia` service of the `installed` and `qualification` modes and the per-checkout `Ohtia Development` service of the `development` mode, followed by the checkout identifier derived from the development root, and the `@napi-rs/keyring` version line in the `2.1.*` form required by `docs/architecture-decisions.md`, used with `linux: { store: 'secret-service' }`; the lockfile and the desktop toolchain record pin 2.1.2. Its consequences: a computer without a usable credential store cannot connect a service; no two processes share a stored session or registration, so renewal needs no lock between processes; and each new development checkout signs in once and takes the API keys again. `docs/adr/index.md` gains its entry with that title and description.

**Decisions and rationale:** The operating-system store keeps provider sessions out of Ohtia's database and out of Electron's platform-dependent `safeStorage`; the researcher rejected a session-only fallback, so a missing store leaves the connection unavailable. Linux is tested on three distinct Secret Service implementations because they behave differently. Main holds every credential and sends every provider request because `architecture.md` gives main the brokered credentials and the application port no credential authority; the data service then never holds a credential, so neither its database, its checkpoints nor its receipts can contain one.[^architecture][^port] Connection operations change only main's connection state, which is idempotent by itself, so they need no receipt. The contract and the credential store are a story of their own because Sign in with ChatGPT and both API-key connections build on them, and the fake provider verifies them before any real connection exists. The new ADR lands here because this story adds `@napi-rs/keyring`, whose version line it owns; the route statements of `architecture.md` and `product-roadmap.md` restate that ADR, so they land with it.

**Verification:** Conformance passes with the fake provider. The artifact inspection test proves that no package contains the fake provider's module. With the fake provider: a repeated connect or disconnect returns the current state and writes no receipt; connect returns `connecting` at once and later states arrive through `connection.subscribe`; and the messages main sends to the data service never contain a credential. On each x86-64 virtual machine, and on GNOME and KDE for Ubuntu 26.04, Fedora 44 and Debian 13 (GNOME Keyring, `ksecretd` and `kwalletd6`), a native test of the credential service in the development runtime stores a synthetic session, reads it back after a restart and deletes it, writing only the entries of its own checkout's `Ohtia Development` service and leaving the installed application's `Ohtia` entries untouched; on Linux a missing store and a cancelled unlock report the store as unavailable and store nothing; on Windows a missing `provider-sessions.enc` reads as `disconnected`, two writes of the same session use different IVs, and a modified file, a file with another version byte, a truncated file, or a file whose key entry was deleted, reads as an unusable session and sets the connection to `expired`. With the fake provider, each connection reason appears only with its state, and a probe returns at once and delivers its outcome and `model_list_revision`, or `unreachable` after 30 seconds without an answer, through `connection.subscribe`, never the list itself; a fake list of 1,500 models, with identifiers and names of up to 256 bytes, crosses the port through `connection.listModels` in pages that each stay within every port bound, joins into the complete list in order, and a page read with an old revision returns `stale_revision` with the current one; a list with an entry too large for one page is never stored. Automated tests run in the 12 CI cells.

**Open questions:** None.

### SPEC-003-007: Sign in with ChatGPT

Implement the connection half of the ChatGPT adapter, Sign in with ChatGPT, on the adapter contract and credential store of STORY-003-001, and its rows in Settings › Connections.[^adr-0026]

Sign in with ChatGPT connection:

- **Declaration.** Under the contract of STORY-003-001, the connection declares the identifier `chatgpt`, the display name "ChatGPT", shown as `{service}`, the kind `subscription` and the usage address `https://chatgpt.com/settings/usage`.
- **Credential store unavailable.** With no store on Linux, which the credential service reports as unavailable (STORY-003-001), Ohtia does not sign in: the connection is `error` with the reason `store_unavailable`, and the row explains how to enable one: "Ohtia keeps your sign-in in your system's keyring, which isn't available. Turn on or unlock GNOME Keyring or KWallet, then sign in again." With a locked store, the system unlock prompt appears; if the researcher cancels it, Ohtia shows the same state and explanation.
- **Registration.** Ohtia uses one ChatGPT account at a time. On first use, Ohtia registers with OpenAI: the authorization request carries the client identifier `dynamic_agent_client`, the `agent_name_hint` "Ohtia" and the host identifier `ext_agent_host_id`, and the callback returns Ohtia's issued client identifier. Before that first sign-in, main generates the host identifier as `urn:uuid:` followed by `crypto.randomUUID()`. When the ID token validates (Callback, below), main stores in the credential-store entry `chatgpt-registration`, separately from the session, the host identifier, the issued client identifier, the account identity: the validated ID token's `sub` claim, which OpenAI names as the account identity, and the account email, the validated ID token's `email` claim, which the rows show as the last account. The email lives only in that operating-system credential-store entry, never in the database, research records or diagnostics; it is replaced only when a new registration replaces the stored one (Another account) and removed only with the registration, so signing out, an expired session and a rejected sign-in keep it. `connection.read` and its events return it as the new field `last_account_email` while a registration is stored, and empty otherwise. Every later sign-in sends the issued client identifier and the same host identifier, without `agent_name_hint`, which OpenAI asks for only on a first registration. A first-registration callback without an issued identifier, or a later one with a different identifier, is rejected with `sign_in_rejected` and changes nothing stored. "Sign out of {service}" does not remove the registration.[^siwc-signin][^siwc-sessions]
- **Sign-in.** Before opening the browser, main starts a listener bound to `127.0.0.1` only, never `localhost`, on a port the operating system assigns, with the redirect address `http://127.0.0.1:{port}/auth/callback`; only the port changes between sign-ins, and the authorization request and the code exchange send the same address. For each attempt, main generates a new random `state`, OIDC `nonce` and PKCE verifier of 32 random bytes each, written in base64url, and opens in the browser `https://auth.openai.com/api/accounts/authorize` with `response_type` `code`, the scopes `openid profile email offline_access resource.invoke chatgpt.tokens.use.direct`, `resource` `https://api.openai.com/v1`, `state`, `nonce`, `code_challenge_method` `S256` and `code_challenge`, the base64url SHA-256 of the verifier without padding. The researcher signs in and authorizes Ohtia to use the plan; Ohtia never sees the password.[^siwc-signin]
- **Callback.** The listener accepts one request: a `GET` of `/auth/callback` whose `state` equals the attempt's. A request for another path is answered 404, and one with a missing or different `state` is answered 400; neither stores anything, exchanges a code or ends the wait. The accepted request closes the listener and is answered with a page in the interface language: "You can close this tab and return to Ohtia." With `error=access_denied`, main exchanges no code and the connection becomes `error` with `plan_use_not_allowed`; with any other `error`, it becomes `error` with `sign_in_rejected`. Otherwise main exchanges the code at `https://auth.openai.com/api/accounts/oauth/token` with the issued client identifier, the verifier, the same redirect address and `resource`, then validates the ID token: its signature against OpenAI's published keys, its issuer, its audience equal to the issued client identifier, its expiration and the attempt's `nonce`. A failed exchange or validation stores nothing and sets `error` with `sign_in_rejected`. A sign-in that passes stores the session, with the refresh token from the token response, and the connection becomes `connected`, unless plan use is missing (below).[^siwc-signin][^siwc-tokens]
- **Waiting for the browser.** While main waits, the connection is `connecting` and its row shows "Finish signing in to {service} in your browser." with a "Cancel sign-in" button. This and the other texts of this connection flow are `subscription` texts; no API-key connection signs in through a browser. "Cancel sign-in" is `connection.disconnect` while `connecting`: it ends the wait and the connection returns to `disconnected` without a message. Main waits at most 10 minutes, the new `sign_in_wait_ms` value 600000 in `config/desktop/resource-policy.json`; then the connection returns to `disconnected` with the reason `sign_in_timeout`, and the row shows "Sign-in wasn't completed within 10 minutes. Try again when you're ready." until the next sign-in or until Ohtia quits (STORY-003-001). Main closes the `127.0.0.1` listener as soon as it accepts the callback, the sign-in is cancelled or the wait times out.
- **Plan use not allowed.** If the researcher declines plan use, the callback carries `error=access_denied`, main exchanges no code and nothing new is stored (Callback); a registration stored by an earlier sign-in stays, and on first use none exists. If the sign-in result lacks the `chatgpt.tokens.use.direct` scope among its granted scopes, Ohtia keeps the sign-in, as OpenAI asks, and sends nothing. In both cases the connection becomes `error` with the reason `plan_use_not_allowed`, and its row shows "Ohtia doesn't have permission to use your {service} plan." with an "Allow plan use" button that starts the sign-in again: with the stored registration when one exists, and otherwise as a first registration, like "Sign in with {service}". Sign-in starts again only when the researcher chooses it.[^siwc-errors]
- **Renewal.** The session renews automatically and persists across restarts until the researcher signs out. Main runs one renewal at a time, so one renewal serves every task (STORY-003-005), and stores each replacement session as soon as it arrives. No other process reads or writes that stored session: the installed application is single-instance per operating-system user, and each development checkout has its own credential-store entries and its own single-instance process (STORY-003-001). So main's copy is always the stored session, and main renews from it without reading the store again and without any lock between processes. OpenAI asks clients to store and use the latest replacement and to serialize renewals so that two processes do not race a rotating token.[^siwc-sessions]
- **Revocation.** OpenAI does not notify revocation; Ohtia detects it when OpenAI rejects a renewal. A renewal answered with `invalid_grant`, `invalid_refresh_token`, `token_expired`, `refresh_token_expired`, `refresh_token_invalidated` or `refresh_token_reused` deletes the unusable session and keeps the registration, and the connection becomes `expired`. A renewal answered with `invalid_client` also deletes the registration, and the connection becomes `error` with `sign_in_rejected`, so the next sign-in registers again. A temporary failure (no network or a 5xx response) never deletes or expires the stored session, and the connection stays `connected`.[^siwc-errors]
- **Another account.** While a registration is stored, the `disconnected` and `expired` rows, and the `error` rows with `plan_use_not_allowed` or `sign_in_rejected`, also offer the secondary button "Sign in with another account", because the issued client stays bound to the account and workspace it registered.[^siwc-signin] It runs `connection.connect` with the new field `new_registration` set to `true`, which starts a first registration as above, with `dynamic_agent_client`, the `agent_name_hint` "Ohtia" and the stored host identifier, under the same sign-in, callback, waiting and plan-use rules. Main keeps this attempt apart from the stored registration until the new ID token validates. Then it replaces `chatgpt-registration` with the new issued client identifier, account identity and account email, keeping the host identifier, and stores the new session in place of any stored one, whichever account the token names. If the attempt fails, is cancelled or times out, the stored registration stays unchanged, and the connection takes the state and reason that the same outcome gives any sign-in. "Sign in as {email}", "Sign in with {service}", "Allow plan use" and every `connection.connect` without `new_registration` keep sending the stored issued client identifier. OpenAI suggests letting the researcher choose among saved accounts; Ohtia keeps one, and never combines one registration's client identifier with another's tokens.[^siwc-sessions]
- **Settings › Connections.** One row per connection, headed by `{service}`, in the order ChatGPT, then the API-key rows of STORY-003-036 and STORY-003-037, Anthropic and OpenAI; its state line and buttons follow its state and reason. `{email}` in a row is the stored registration's `last_account_email`, and "Sign in as {email}", the primary button, runs `connection.connect` with the stored registration; "Sign in with another account" is always the secondary button beside it.
  - `disconnected` with no stored registration: "Not connected", or, with `sign_in_timeout`, the timeout text above as plain text, with the primary "Sign in with {service}".
  - `disconnected` while a registration is stored: "Not connected · Last account: {email}", or, with `sign_in_timeout`, the timeout text above as plain text followed by the line "Last account: {email}", with "Sign in as {email}" and "Sign in with another account".
  - `connecting`: the waiting row above.
  - `connected`: "Connected · {email}", where `{email}` is the session's account email, with "Test connection" and "Sign out of {service}", which deletes the stored session and keeps the registration; a connected row never shows a sign-in button.
  - `expired`: the attention notice "Your {service} session expired. Sign in again.", the line "Last account: {email}" when a registration is stored, and "Sign in as {email}" and "Sign in with another account", or "Sign in with {service}" when none is stored.
  - `error` with `store_unavailable`: the keyring explanation above with "Sign in with {service}" and no last account, because no stored registration can be read.
  - `error` with `plan_use_not_allowed`: the plan-use row above, whose primary button stays "Allow plan use", with the line "Last account: {email}" and "Sign in with another account" while a registration is stored; with no stored registration, as after `error=access_denied` on first use, the row shows only "Allow plan use", without a last account.
  - `error` with `sign_in_rejected`, when OpenAI rejected the sign-in, the grant or the registration (STORY-003-003): "Ohtia can't use this {service} sign-in. Sign in again.", with the line "Last account: {email}", "Sign in as {email}" and "Sign in with another account" while a registration is stored, so a rejected attempt with a new account can be repeated, or "Sign in with {service}" when none is stored.
  - "Sign in with {service}", "Sign in as {email}" and "Allow plan use" open the browser, so each carries the Lucide `external-link` icon, whose accessible name is "opens in your browser". The usage pause has no text in this row; it shows in the chat's notices (STORY-003-013). On Windows, the `expired` row also appears when the credential service finds the session file but not its key, or the file fails to decrypt, while a missing file reads "Not connected" (STORY-003-001). The `expired` and `error` rows and the keyring explanation are attention notices with `triangle-alert`; every state has its text, so none relies on color. These are the `subscription` rows; STORY-003-036 adds the `api_key` rows.
- **Test.** "Test connection" runs `connection.probe` and lists the account's models: only entries with `visibility` equal to `list`, shown by their `display_name`; requests send the entry's `slug`.[^siwc-models] Sign in with ChatGPT exposes no plan, entitlement or eligibility data before a request: its model list, token reference and error pages document none.[^siwc-tokens][^siwc-errors] While the test runs, until its probe-result event arrives through `connection.subscribe` (STORY-003-001), the status "Testing the connection with {service}…" is announced politely. "Test connection" therefore reports, in one status notice with `info` below the row's buttons, separately "Your {service} sign-in works.", "Models: {models}", where `{models}` is the list of display names in the order received, read completely with `connection.listModels` pages under the probe's `model_list_revision` (STORY-003-001) and joined as a list in the interface language, and the line "{service} doesn't say in advance whether your plan can be used in Ohtia. The first reply will show it."; when the service cannot be reached, or does not answer within `connection_probe_ms`, it keeps the session and shows a danger notice with `octagon-alert`: "Ohtia couldn't reach {service}. Your sign-in is kept; check your internet connection and test again." An account that cannot use its plan fails its first reply with `not_eligible` (STORY-003-003).

**Documentation amendments:**

- `config/desktop/resource-policy.json`: add the budget `sign_in_wait_ms` with `value` 600000, `unit` `millisecond`, permitted minimum and maximum 600000, `owner` `003`, the port contract's owner, as every existing budget has, scope "The browser sign-in of one connection, from connect until the sign-in returns", failure `sign_in_timeout` and rationale "A researcher may need several minutes to sign in and authorize plan use". `scripts/desktop_contract.py` gains the name and its unit.

**Decisions and rationale:** The browser wait is a subscription with a 10-minute bound and a "Cancel sign-in", because a sign-in can take minutes and the port's request deadline is 5 seconds. PKCE, `state`, `nonce`, the `127.0.0.1` address with its fixed path and the ID-token checks are what OpenAI's registration and sign-in page requires; accepting one matching request and ignoring the rest keeps another local program from binding Ohtia to its own account through the listener.[^siwc-signin] OpenAI saves each issued client identifier with the registration of one ChatGPT account, reuses it only for that account and binds it to its registered workspace, so another account or workspace needs a first registration; Ohtia keeps one account at a time, and the new registration replaces the stored one only after its ID token validates, as OpenAI asks to keep a new sign-in apart from the active account until its identity is validated, so a failed or cancelled attempt never loses the working registration.[^siwc-signin][^siwc-sessions] OpenAI does not notify revocation, so a rejected renewal is the only signal, and a temporary failure keeps the stored session so that a network outage never signs the researcher out.[^siwc-errors] Renewal needs no lock between processes because no two processes share a stored session: the installed application is single-instance per operating-system user, and each development checkout has its own credential-store entries (STORY-003-001), so OpenAI's rule that renewals be serialized holds with main's one renewal at a time. The cost is one sign-in in each new checkout.[^architecture][^siwc-sessions] "Test connection" says that plan eligibility shows only in the first reply because Sign in with ChatGPT documents no eligibility data before a request.[^siwc-tokens][^siwc-errors] The researcher asked, at the mockup review, that a disconnected row remember the last account, so that "Sign in as {email}" says which account the stored registration signs in to; the email stays with the registration in the operating-system credential store, beside the other sign-in data, and never reaches the database.[^mockups] Linux is tested on three distinct Secret Service implementations because they behave differently.

**Verification:** Conformance passes with ChatGPT. On each x86-64 virtual machine, and on GNOME and KDE for Ubuntu 26.04, Fedora 44 and Debian 13 (GNOME Keyring, `ksecretd` and `kwalletd6`), the researcher signs in and the real model list appears; the session survives a restart and "Sign out of {service}" ends it while the registration remains; "Cancel sign-in" while connecting and a declined plan use each show their state and message; every connection state shows its row text and buttons, and "Test connection" announces its progress and shows its result; on Linux a missing store and a cancelled unlock show their message; on Windows a deleted session file shows "Not connected" and a modified one the `expired` row; signing in from the development runtime writes only the entries of the checkout's own `Ohtia Development` service and leaves the installed application's `Ohtia` entries untouched. With the fake provider: the authorization request carries a new `state`, `nonce` and S256 `code_challenge` for each attempt, the listener answers only on `127.0.0.1`, and the code exchange sends the verifier and the same redirect address; a forged callback with a missing or different `state`, a request for another path and a second request after the accepted one store nothing and exchange no code, and the first two leave the wait running; `error=access_denied`, a failed exchange, an ID token with a wrong signature, issuer, audience, expiration or `nonce`, a first registration without an issued identifier and a later one with a different identifier each leave their state and reason and store nothing; a first registration stores the host identifier, the issued identifier, the ID token's `sub` and its `email` claim in `chatgpt-registration`, the email appears in no database row, research record or diagnostic, and a later sign-in sends the stored identifiers without `agent_name_hint`; while a registration is stored and the connection is not connected, the rows show "Last account: {email}" from `last_account_email` with "Sign in as {email}", which signs in with the stored registration, and "Sign in with another account", sign-out and expiry keep the remembered email, a validated new account replaces it, `store_unavailable` shows no remembered account and only "Sign in with {service}", `plan_use_not_allowed` keeps "Allow plan use", and no connected row shows a sign-in button; "Sign in with another account" appears in the `disconnected` and `expired` rows and in the `plan_use_not_allowed` and `sign_in_rejected` `error` rows only while a registration is stored, sends `dynamic_agent_client`, `agent_name_hint` "Ohtia" and the stored host identifier, and with a second fake account whose ID token validates replaces the stored registration, with the new issued identifier, `sub` and email and the same host identifier, and the session, after which "Sign in with {service}" sends the new issued identifier; the same flow cancelled, timed out, with `error=access_denied`, a failed exchange or an invalid ID token leaves the stored registration unchanged, and the next "Sign in with {service}" sends the earlier issued identifier; the 10-minute wait ends in `disconnected` with `sign_in_timeout`, its message and a closed listener, and on first use that row offers only "Sign in with {service}"; `error=access_denied` on first use stores no registration and its row shows only "Allow plan use", which then starts a first registration; each refresh error code leaves its state and reason; two development runtimes running at the same time from two development roots, signed in to two fake accounts, store their sessions and registrations under two distinct services, `Ohtia Development` followed by each root's own checkout identifier (STORY-003-001), each reads back and renews only its own, and signing out of one leaves the other's session in place; a run in the `installed` mode, with `OHTIA_DEVELOPMENT_ROOT` set in its environment, signs in, renews and signs out reading and writing only entries of the service `Ohtia`, never one whose service begins with `Ohtia Development`; a temporary renewal failure keeps the stored session and the `connected` state; and an unreachable service, or a probe without an answer for 30 seconds, keeps the session and shows its notice. Automated tests run in the 12 CI cells.

**Open questions:** None.

### SPEC-003-008: ChatGPT requests

Complete the adapter contract with the request lifecycle and implement ChatGPT requests over the Responses API.[^adr-0026][^adr-0028][^siwc-limitations]

Generic contract:

- **Request operations.** Start, interrupt and query (the architecture's `inspect`). Resume stays in the contract; ChatGPT declares it unsupported because Sign in with ChatGPT does not store responses, so every request carries all the context it needs.
- **Request data.** Attempt identity, operation, connection, model, inputs and limits, never a credential. STORY-003-005 adds the project, the policy and instruction versions and the document language when real tasks replace the development attempt identity; STORY-003-014 adds the permitted tools.
- **Events.** Each with a sequence number: started, partial text, reported usage and final result. STORY-003-014 adds the tool-call event.
- **Final results.** Completed; failed with its reason; cancelled; uncertain, when the connection dropped after dispatch and Ohtia cannot know whether the provider processed the request. A failure carries one of the reasons `usage_limit_reached`, `not_eligible`, `connection_expired`, `provider_unavailable`, `request_rejected`, `provider_error`, `invalid_output` and `connection_lost`; STORY-003-005 stores it in `attempts.outcome_reason`. When main sends nothing, the result is marked not dispatched, with `connection_expired`, `provider_unavailable` or the reason `permit_expired`, used only for this case (below).
- **Dispatch control.** Just before sending, the data service checks that the connection is still active, records the exact list of what it sends (references, never content, and never in diagnostics) and issues a single-use dispatch permit valid for 2 seconds. Ohtia never retries on its own. In this story the list is returned with the attempt result; STORY-003-005 stores it in `attempts.transfer_manifest` in the dispatch transaction, which also writes `attempts.dispatch_permitted_at` and commits before the data service posts `provider_dispatch`. The 2-second validity is a versioned constant that the real-account checks measure, as ADR-0028 requires; it is not a response deadline.[^adr-0028]
- **Between the data service and main.** They exchange versioned, runtime-validated messages, defined in `packages/contracts`, over the existing service channel (`process.parentPort` in the data service, supervised by `ServiceSupervisor` in `packages/main/src/service-supervisor.ts`):
  - `provider_dispatch`, from the data service: its `generation`, the attempt identity, the connection identifier, the request data and the dispatch permit. Main discards, without a result, a dispatch from an ended generation and one whose permit it has already used. Otherwise it works in this order: it checks that the permit was issued less than 2 seconds earlier for that attempt; renews the session once if it has expired (Session renewal, below); checks that the connection is still connected, and otherwise sends nothing and returns the attempt as not dispatched with `connection_expired`, which STORY-003-006 handles as a rejected renewal; checks the permit's age again just before the body leaves; and only then attaches the credential and sends the request. These are ADR-0028's guards before the transport is invoked and before the body is released.[^adr-0028] A permit that is 2 seconds old or more at either check is refused: nothing is sent, and main returns the attempt as not dispatched with the reason `permit_expired`, which STORY-003-005 turns into the task's second attempt.
  - `provider_event`, from main: the attempt identity and each provider event with its sequence number, ending with the final result. The data service stores them; main keeps no copy after forwarding.
  - `provider_interrupt`, from the data service: the attempt identity. Main closes that request's connection and forwards the cancelled result.
  - When a data-service generation ends, for any reason, main closes every provider connection that generation dispatched and discards their later events before it starts the next data service, so the restart review (STORY-003-008) never meets a request of the old generation that is still streaming.
- **Usage.** Tokens reported by the provider are recorded; unreported values stay unknown.

ChatGPT:

- **Transport.** Main sends the requests, using the Responses API with `store: false` and `stream: true`; history goes in `input` and operation instructions in `instructions`, because system-role messages are rejected. Unsupported fields such as `background`, `max_output_tokens`, `metadata`, `temperature`, `truncation` and `user` are never sent. Success requires `response.completed`.[^siwc-limitations][^siwc-models]
- **No provider memory.** A request carries only the instructions, input and tools that Ohtia builds. With `store: false` it leaves no stored response, and it never sends `previous_response_id`, `conversation` or `prompt`, which would bring in conversation state or prompts kept by the provider, nor any tool other than Ohtia's own. The conformance suite checks every request for these fields, and the marker sweep of STORY-003-034 checks that nothing said in one project reaches a reply in another.
- **Interrupt.** Closes the response; the result is cancelled.
- **Errors and outcomes.** OpenAI's documented errors map to these results; no outcome repeats a request by itself, and OpenAI does not silently switch to another billing path.[^siwc-errors]
  - Before the stream opens, the body may be `{"detail":"..."}`, diagnostic text rather than a code, which Ohtia never shows or stores. A 401 fails with `connection_expired`, and main renews the session once: the connection becomes `expired` only when that renewal fails with an unusable-token code (STORY-003-002), and otherwise stays `connected`. A 403, a policy or permission block such as the serving region, fails with `not_eligible`. A 503 fails with `provider_unavailable` and keeps the credentials.
  - `subscription_sharing_user_not_eligible` (403) fails with `not_eligible`; Ohtia neither repeats it nor starts a new sign-in.
  - `subscription_sharing_usage_limit_exceeded` (429) fails with `usage_limit_reached`; STORY-003-006 opens the plan pause. Ohtia never assumes the plan is empty or states when it renews.
  - `subscription_sharing_usage_unavailable` (503) and `subscription_sharing_user_unavailable` (503) fail with `provider_unavailable` and keep the credentials.
  - `subscription_sharing_unsupported_capability` (400) and `subscription_sharing_route_not_supported` (403) fail with `request_rejected`; Ohtia never sends the same body again by itself.
  - `subscription_sharing_invalid_user` (401) is handled as a 401 before the stream: `connection_expired` and one renewal.
  - `chatpass_v2_scope_not_authorized` and `chatpass_v2_invalid_authorization_context` (403) fail with `request_rejected`, and the connection becomes `error` with the reason `sign_in_rejected`, stored with the kept session, so its queued tasks block until the researcher signs in again and a new grant replaces the old one.
  - Any other 4xx fails with `request_rejected`; any other 429 and any other 5xx fail with `provider_unavailable`.
  - In the stream, `response.completed` completes the reply. `response.failed`, `response.incomplete` or an `error` event fails with `provider_error`, keeping the text already received as partial (STORY-003-007). An event or output that Ohtia cannot parse fails with `invalid_output`; main closes the response, and the text already received is kept as partial (STORY-003-007).
  - A stream that ends without `response.completed`, `response.failed` or `response.incomplete`, or a connection that drops after the request left, is uncertain with `connection_lost`. A connection that could not be opened at all (name resolution, refused connection or TLS failure), so no request byte left, fails with `provider_unavailable`.
- **Session renewal.** Main renews an expired session once before dispatch, between the two permit checks above. If OpenAI rejects the renewal with an unusable-token code or `invalid_client` (STORY-003-002), the connection becomes `expired` or `error` as that story states and nothing is sent: main returns the attempt as not dispatched with `connection_expired`, and STORY-003-006 turns that into a block. If the renewal fails temporarily (no network or a 5xx response), the connection stays `connected` and nothing is sent: main returns the attempt as not dispatched with `provider_unavailable`, and the task fails with that reason (STORY-003-006).

Development command and `development_test` policy:

- **Surface.** In the `development` runtime mode of ADR-0043, and only there, main admits the switch `--ohtia-dev-command=<action>` with its arguments as `--ohtia-dev-<argument>=<value>` switches; in the `installed` and `qualification` modes the existing command-line admission (`admitRuntimeSwitches`) refuses them like any other override.[^adr-0043] `python3 scripts/workspace.py run dev` passes these switches to the runtime. While the checkout's window is open, the new launch hands them to the open instance through Electron's `second-instance` event, so an action can reach a reply that is streaming. Each action calls the same operation as the interface action it stands in for, with its own operation identifier, and prints the identifiers it creates and its results to the terminal of the running instance.
